### **@williamvandongen9591** | 👍 66 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgwpFHJsnY8_xP3wwON4AaABAg)

What really stood out to me in this video is the idea that having fewer resources can actually be an advantage, because it forces efficiency, discipline, and long‑term strategy rather than complacency. I think this logic doesn’t just apply to states or empires, but very clearly to individuals as well.

If you translate this to real life, you often see that the kids with the least social capital in school — the unpopular kids, nerds, and geeks — end up doing disproportionately well later in life. Not because they are “better” people, but because they have no alternative strategy. They can’t rely on popularity, looks, or networks, so they invest heavily in skills, knowledge, and competence. In game‑theory terms: their payoff matrix pushes them toward delayed but scalable rewards.

Meanwhile, people with abundant early resources (status, validation, social dominance) often optimize for short‑term gains. That works early on, but it creates fragility. When the environment changes — adulthood, labor markets, technology shifts — those advantages don’t compound the same way.

So just like in the “world game” described here, the party with fewer resources is forced to play a leaner, more adaptive, and more future‑oriented strategy. Over time, that strategy tends to dominate. Scarcity doesn’t guarantee success, but it strongly selects for behaviors that win in the long run.

That’s why it often looks, in hindsight, like the “losers” ended up conquering the world.

### **@yahyabelghiti-alaoui1468** | 👍 256 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgymxxEyA-r_G9w6PNV4AaABAg)

The concept introduced by Ibn Khaldoun is Assabiyah (عصبية). Ibn Khaldoun states that people who live in the margins of the empire (nomadic tribes from the sahara desert and steppes people) have more "Assabiah" than the sedentary people who live in the empire which means that they tend to be more violent more strict and more motivated than the sedentary people of the empire. 
Ibn Khaldoun developed this idea in a very specific historic context : during the 14th century, north african cities (like Fez and Qayraouan) were subject to attacks and sacks from nomadic tribes coming from the arabian desert (Banu Hilal, Banu Maaqil and Banu Sulaym).
Ibn Khaldoun also says that, eventually, the margins conquer the empire and they do the exact same thing as their predecessors (they become sedentary, lazy, used to "soft life"), this was very true especially in the muslim word (the almoravids, almohads, merinids, hafsids, zianids, seljuks, etc followed almost the exact same pattern).

### **@carshowcasingcsc**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=Ugw5CZIVJQkw5d0Gy3F4AaABAg)

0:00
Core question: Why do states rise and fall?
Sets up the entire lecture. Frames history as a repeatable pattern, not random events.

0:49
Why “strong” states usually lose
Introduces the paradox. The richest, most powerful states almost never become the final empire.

2:11
Qin example (China): the weakest state wins
Explains why Qin, geographically poor and isolated, unified China despite worse starting conditions.

3:11
Ibn Khaldun and Asabiyyah (cohesion)
Introduces the key concept: marginalized groups win because they are more unified.

4:16
The three metrics of civilizational power
Energy, openness, cohesion. This becomes the operating model for the rest of the lecture.

5:36
Why rich societies decay
Wealth leads to low energy, arrogance, corruption, and fragmentation.

7:02
Greek city-states and Macedonia
Why Athens should have won and why Macedonia actually did.

9:34
Alexander and the Persian Empire
Shows how a small, cohesive force can defeat an enormous empire quickly.

11:26
Aztecs: from starvation to empire
How extreme constraint forces creativity and cohesion.

12:49
500 Spaniards defeat millions
Introduces the problem standard explanations don’t fully solve.

14:21
Game theory lens introduced
Shifts from historical facts to incentives, players, and strategy.

18:06
The first game: cooperation and religion
Early civilizations use belief systems to coordinate energy and meaning.

19:26
Elite heredity and bureaucratic religion
Religion turns from motivation into control.

20:25
Elite overproduction
Too many elites competing for status leads to internal conflict.

21:28
Warring States = peak creativity
Open competition drives innovation and cultural explosions.

23:24
Intermarriage and equilibrium
Elites stabilize power, competition collapses into maintenance mode.

24:09
War as population control
War stops being about winning and starts being about managing elites.

25:45
Court politics: secrecy, trust, coordination
Defines the mechanics of factional struggle inside empires.

26:46
Rise of secret societies
How factions survive and compete without being destroyed.

29:05
Empires rot from the inside
Insularity, corruption, division. Direct inversion of the three metrics.

31:26
Mercenaries invited in
Elites outsource violence. This is the beginning of the end.

32:48
Mercenaries take over
The pattern repeats across China, Greece, Rome, and beyond.

33:48
Predicting the future with the model
Find the most energetic, open, cohesive groups. They win next.

34:14
The World Game (classroom simulation)
Real-world demonstration of the theory.

36:40
Why Pakistan often finishes #2
Starting with nothing forces creativity, lying, stealing, cooperation.

37:44
Poverty as a competitive advantage
Scarcity produces adaptability faster than abundance.

37:50
Provocative forecast: North Korea vs China
Purely theoretical conclusion based on game dynamics, not morality.

---

### **@lussiez100** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=Ugwk51aJ04KY_scZ8L54AaABAg)

I like him, but his suggestion does not make much sense. He argued that Japan or Germany could become the next great empire, which is highly unconvincing.

Both countries have some of the worst demographic trends in the world, with aging and declining populations. This is the exact opposite of what characterizes an energetic and rising society. In terms of openness, they have also largely missed the 21st-century digital revolution: most of their leading industries still belong to the 19th or 20th century (automobiles, chemistry, heavy industry), which again reflects a lack of structural openness to new technological paradigms.

As for cohesion, one can observe a clear increase in social and cultural tensions over the last decade, particularly in Germany, between ethnic Germans and populations of Turkish or Syrian origin.

From a game-theory or strategic-competition perspective, this hypothesis is therefore not very convincing. Countries such as Vietnam, Turkey, or India fit the historical profile of rising powers much better.

24:39 - Court politics shift focus from meritocracy to maintaining power structures.
28:54 - Factions within empires lead to insularity, corruption, and division.
31:21 - Mercenaries influence power dynamics in factions through trade, theft, and warfare.
35:40 - Resourcefulness and teamwork can outperform sheer wealth in global dynamics.
37:42 - Poverty fosters creativity and influences future potential in East Asia.
41:33 - Success can stem from humility and teamwork rather than arrogance.
43:56 - Societies that fall often do not return to greatness.
47:42 - The theory suggests resource-poor nations can succeed through creativity and resilience.
49:27 - Wealthy nations can avoid corruption and decline through external power dynamics.
53:20 - Leadership is crucial for transforming poor societies into unified powers.
55:23 - The emergence of leadership affects global stability and development.

### **@EliasFaizi-m3d**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgxUh0DSU_jJYWRPDIp4AaABAg)

First of all, thank you for your lectures and the work you do on this channel. I’ve watched many of your videos, and I genuinely appreciate how open-minded, structured, and honest your approach to history and geopolitics is. In a time where many young people are searching for real knowledge and deeper understanding beyond surface-level narratives, your lectures are truly valuable and rare.

One thought I wanted to share respectfully: when discussing history, you often and rightly include the Muslim world and Islam, given the immense role it has played in shaping global history. However, when speaking about the present and especially future geopolitical developments, I notice that the Muslim world is mentioned far less, if at all.

This feels a bit incomplete to me, as I strongly believe the Muslim world still holds significant potential and will continue to play an important role in future global dynamics—politically, culturally, and strategically. I would personally find it very enriching if future lectures also explored this dimension when discussing upcoming global shifts.

Thank you again for your work and for encouraging critical thinking and genuine learning.

### **@geosenseagroproject987**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgxQiEQfomKG4MjimE94AaABAg)

"game theory" of a nation evolves through specific stages:
The Startup Phase: Driven by a "dynamic religion" or mythology. People are enthusiastic and cohesive.
The Bureaucratic Phase: Privileges become hereditary. Religion/rules are used to protect the status quo rather than inspire creativity.
Elite Overproduction: Too many people fighting for too few top positions. This leads to factions.
The Equilibrium (The Trap): Factions care more about defeating internal rivals than external threats.
Secret Societies: Factions use secrecy, "transgression" (shared crimes/rituals), and mythology to coordinate.
The Mercenary Trap: Internal factions invite "barbarians" (mercenaries) to help them win domestic disputes. These mercenaries eventually realize they are stronger than their employers and take over.

Humanity—what is humanity?
​We are different from one another.
Different cultures.
Different food.
Different languages.
Different skin colors.
Different ways of ruling.
​But humanity is more than all of the above.
We may be different from one another, but the core is not.
​We all have a mother.
We all have a father.
Sisters, brothers, uncles, aunts, nieces, sons, daughters...
We should care for one another based on these common traits.
​I will care for your child if they are stranded.
I will care for your brother if he is harmed.
I will help your old mother if she needs help crossing the street.
​Not because it is "the right thing to do,"
But because she is a mother—just like my mother.
​Be silent with words.
Be loud with kindness.

### **@katiSanPsema** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=Ugz9ATS7qQBipN7MLf54AaABAg)

A lot of these “rise and fall of empires” narratives treat history like a chessboard. States move, elites strategize, one power conquers another, and the story advances as if societies were single players with unified interests.
What’s missing is the perspective of the people who are not playing the game.
In most cases, it’s not a people conquering another people. It’s one elite replacing another elite, then imposing new rules, new identities, and new myths on the same population. The borders change, the symbols change, the language of legitimacy changes, but for common people the pattern is familiar: more extraction, more obligations, less autonomy.
Empire at its “peak” is often when ordinary life gets worse. Roman citizens lived better during the Republic than under the Empire, when taxation rose, small farmers were ruined, and power concentrated in a narrow class. The same applies to many imperial systems, including the Ottomans at their height, where stability for the state meant heavier burdens for the population.
These theories focus heavily on how elites persuade, coordinate, and outmaneuver each other. They talk about cohesion, energy, myth-making, and identity as tools for winning the game. But they rarely pause to ask who pays the cost of that cohesion, or who benefits from those myths. Nations, races, civilizations, and destinies often function less as organic identities and more as instruments to mobilize people for goals they did not choose.
From the ground level, the question is not “who will rule the world next?”
It’s “how much are we ruled, by whom, and at what cost?”
If power simply rotates between different elites, then conquest is not progress, just rebranding. For most people, it doesn’t matter whether the ruler speaks Latin, Ottoman Turkish, Mandarin, or something else, if the structure remains one of extraction and control.
Maybe the more important historical question is not which empire wins, but how ordinary people can limit how much power any elite holds over them in the first place. Because from that perspective, the real struggle is not between civilizations, but between those who rule and those who are ruled.
And that part of the story is almost always left off the board

### **@second-order-consequences** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=Ugwv558ihLNRMBe04It4AaABAg)

Professor Jiang’s framework seems to rely on two claims that feel inconsistent.

Claim 1: Power is driven by elite coordination (often secretive). Ordinary people are mostly pawns.
Claim 2: Open, cohesive, energetic societies tend to beat arrogant empires.

How can both be true?

When we say “Japan” or “Germany” will rise, who is the “open/cohesive/energetic” actor: society as a whole, or the ruling elite and institutions or the peasant class? If Claim 1 is right, the decisive unit seems to be elites, not the public.

Also, if a dominant empire is rational, why wouldn’t it block rivals from staying open and cohesive? If the U.S. saw Japan rising again, why would they not destroy just their economy but the reason why they became strong economies (openness/cohesion/energy)? Moreover, if the secretive elites only care about their own interest, and they rule all societies, how is it possible to say which nation comes up on top as it is a matter of great indifference to the people who rule the world? 

Finally, the “population trap” argument suggests Japan is structurally limited (aging, low birth rate), so I’m not sure it even fits as a rising-power example.

I get that these are models, not laws, but even at the first level of analysis, they seem to contradict. What’s the correct way to reconcile them?

1- Is the mind=reason independent of 'Time'? How did it arise? Is the mind immortal or mortal?

1.0- Is something outside of reason possible? Or how did reason arise?

Simply put, if we define the initial state as nothingness, emptiness, or darkness, which means it is out of reach for us, what would be the first thing created or existing? Would 'Change,' in the sense of transitioning to a 'new state,' be the first thing created? If we equate 'Change' with time, would time be the first thing created? But wouldn't what we call 'the state before the first change' also be created? The 'initial state' must be one and whole, it must not carry contradictions or dualities, it must be like the 'Sun' in a symbolic sense, everything must be equal, it must not carry the duality of light and darkness within it, it must not carry the duality of night and day. Because 'Change' first leads to differentiations in the whole, and from these, 'Dualities = Opposites' begin to emerge. And the First Duality becomes the first principle of the Mind.

If so, 'Prior State = Sun' is the first creation, 'Change = Time' is the second creation, and 'Prior Duality = Mind' is the third creation.

'The first state' contains no contradictions or differences; it is one and whole, equal, without imbalance; 'Pure Peace' resides in it, and in short, it is 'Pure Beauty'. And 'Pure Beauty' precedes time and reason. Everything originates from 'Pure Beauty', and beauty easily permeates everything, but reason cannot. Beauty can stop time, accelerate and slow it down. And beauty can destroy reason and recreate it. In short, 'Pure Beauty' corresponds to the angel Tawus. It shares many similarities with the Christian concept of Jesus and also with the Hindu concept of 'Atman'.

1.0.0- The context of derivation-emanation and creation;

In the cause-and-effect chain, we prefer to use the expression 'emanation' for things we can explain, but 'creation' for situations beyond the reach of the logical chain, or 'emanation' for the 'phenomenon field' and 'creation' for the 'noumena field', or 'creation' in the context of 'infinite regression', but 'emanation' for standard reasoning, or creation for singularities, emanation for standard situations.

The main reason for that is there are infinite steps between the first known thing and the first principle, so the first principle of our existence is not the ultimate the first principle. The ultimate principle is so transcendent that it stands outside our ontological horizon entirely.

Every mind is based on many fundamental judgments, or opposites—night and day, or light and darkness—which are fundamentally based on a linear presupposition of time and space. But 'actual events' can never be fully encompassed by any mind; that is, a mind independent of time and space is impossible. This makes it inevitable for our linear minds to encounter situations that appear profoundly vertical and therefore causeless, situations that break our chain of reason. For our minds, these events become singularities, things that come from infinite depths. Thus, every mind is doomed to perish. However, since life without a mind is impossible, either the mind is reformed with patch-like solutions that also explain the new causeless situation, or a new mind emerges through more revolutionary transformations called enlightenment.
Therefore, Reason is fundamentally concerned with the realm of 'Phenomena' – the shadows in Plato's cave – and in situations that overwhelm the mind, a connection is made with 'Noumena,' meaning that Light begins to enter, and the darkness, night, or shadows upon which established judgments are based disappear, thus annihilating the contradictions.

From all of this, Noumena is 'Pure Beauty' and corresponds to the classical concept of 'Soul,' possessing an existence independent of 'Time,' meaning it is immortal in the classical sense. What is created is fundamentally Noumena, that is, Pure Beauty, or Soul, or Light. The reflections of created things that occur within us are Phenomena, which is the realm of the Mind, and these are fundamentally the time-dependent derivatives or emanations of 'Light and Darkness'  - Schrödinger's Cat -.

Because people naturally believe in and worship 'Power'. Here, 'Powerlessness' takes on a meaning like 'Being Without Judgment' because 'Pure Beauty' is about being without judgment.  
>

Absolute judgments cease to be a realm of thought. They become immutable, establishing definitive boundaries. Just as walls protect a city, these protect 'identity' from external influences.  
>   
> Furthermore, 'Being Without Judgment' is beneficial for development because it adapts to change very quickly, while 'Being With Judgment' is necessary for self-preservation. Therefore, there should be a balance between what requires judgment and what requires non-judgment, and this balance varies from person to person and from society to society.  
>   
> 'Judging' carries power, but the rate of change of power is far greater than that of reason. So, whose child is 'Power'? Is power truly only the opposite of Pure Beauty? 'Reason' is a possible cause, but is it the direct cause? 'Reason' and 'Power' are subject to 'Time,' that is, they are subject to change. But 'Power' is not solely subject to reason, the judgment that the most intelligent is the most powerful should not be true. For example, 'Emotion = Pathos,' sacred things and taboos —let's assume these stem from definite and fixed judgments and are based on the duality of hot and cold— should be linked to 'Power.' Therefore, from this perspective, power is greater in emotion which has in its core very strong and almost undisputable judgments —more unchanging—, compared to thinking. Perhaps power is change itself.  
>   
> In the jungle, the strongest animal is the lion, because it is the most deadly. Similarly, the most powerful state in the world is the American state, which possesses the most deadly weapons, yet it does not possess the most successful statecraft. Perhaps, then, the illusion of 'Power' is closely linked to 'Life and Death'. Perhaps, then, Life is the mother of the illusion of Power.  
>   
> 'Pure Beauty' already contains within itself 'All Mind = Light' and 'All Emotions = Heat'.  
>   
> In Islam and Judaism, Allah-Yahweh directly means 'Pure Power,' while in Christianity, although it refers to 'Pure Beauty,' there is a similar underlying belief. For example, Allah's creation is described as "He says 'Be,' and it is." In short, 'Pure Power.' The fear of death in humans makes belief in 'Pure Power' inevitable. Furthermore, in Islam and Judaism, the prophet Moses committed an act that would be directly punishable as murder in any modern legal system, and the prophets Muhammad and Moses commanded many battles – warlords from the perspective of classical ideal morality – meaning they are not defined as individuals who have transcended worldly calculations in the classical sense – they neither became Buddha nor attained Nirvana, meaning they did not transcend the classical worldly mind bound to time and space – thus transforming the classical concept of the ideal human being.  
>   
> The ideal human type is transformed from the "non-judgmental" type, based on the principle of "retribution" and the slogan of justice, to a new, highly altered "judgmental" type, characterized by the obligation to "attack injustice and the enemy." This new ideal human type differs significantly from the old ideal human type, who seemed guaranteed to lose everything in the face of conflict, thus creating a new type of human who seems guaranteed to win in life. In other words, the new "Ideal Human" has a solution for establishing a balance between "being non-judgmental" and "being judgmental." Or, the ideal human being who is a little bit of everything, the new ideal morality, as seen through the lens of the old ideal morality, is simply both good and bad, both right and wrong, both beautiful and ugly, both war and peace, but most importantly, a "winner" character.  
>   
> And this new mindset is closer to the mindset of the Romans. It is hardly like the mindsets of the Manicheans, Zoroastrians, Hindus, Buddhists, or Taoists. In the conditions under which the ideal morality of that day was practiced, the fundamental argument behind such a mindset's claim to be ideal is the concept of God-Yahweh-Jesus, it has no claim to philosophical depth, and it represents a sharp break from the fundamental virtues that humanity has developed throughout history. This mindset is not an alternative new mindset that emerged from the fundamental intellectual circles of that day, it completely breaks with tradition, seeking obedience through a new, unquestionable concept of its own making, without providing any rational basis. And there has never been a greater revolution in history than this. This cannot be achieved through violence, lies, deception, or anything similar, it requires a far, far deeper intelligence, and because this intelligence operates so profoundly that it is no longer perceptible to other average minds, the illusion of a supernatural intelligence has been created. From this, we can say that they were not sufficiently 'Powerful,' and by analogy, not sufficiently 'Judgmental'. They do not have said something over 'Noumena field', but only 'Phenomena field'. Christianity seems closer to the Roman Greek reason, while Islam appears closer to the Roman Latin reason -Plotinus, Porphyry,... -. And the reference animal of the Roman mind is the wolf, perhaps the most terrifying beast of nature.

### **@hardlYIncognito**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgzRphYKcMo-C0XYthZ4AaABAg)

I feel your points made sense until brought to light of the modern world. 

Germany, while not in "power", has had their peoples suffer the fate of the West as your student mentioned. I'd be hard pressed to say they are energetic (their migrant class may be), cohesive (remember, being "German" is largely looked down upon), but they certainly are open.

Japan lacks energy, as they are currently exhibiting a negative birthrate at this time. Their openness to new cultures/peoples is "non-existent" (even tourists are loathed), but their cohesion is second to none.

Finally Israel, while they are the most promising of the 3 I feel they are technically the "incumbent" power. They've clearly ran the US post-WWII. I do see them as energetic (large families, younger population), cohesive (their high-trust culture exhibits this) and they are very open (lots of starts ups, intermingling of ideas).

So I only agree your 3rd selection fits your given criteria. I see a lot of hubris with the Israeli government, so even if they do take lead, it won't take long for arrogance to beseech them. 

Also - you're sleeping on Southeast Asia. They are highly energetic (lots of youth), very cohesive (traditional Buddhist culture) and exhibit openness to new ideas. I'd wager a Southeast Asian nation over Japan/Germany, but that's just me. 

There's also a high probability that while the USA may fall, the new "nation" that arises isn't the same. It could be a technofeudal nation, a christian nationalist or maybe even governed as a wider extension of the Israeli super power. 

After all, if secret societies are the true leaders, then it's their "power structures" that never raise again. Meaning, that the US elite may fall (and it won't be pretty) but another fledgling nation with a new upstart elite could take hold, and all those weapons aren't going to simply "disappear" into thin air....

### **@0xkillswitch** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgzwgJHPAy2PTlu1JJB4AaABAg)

It would be nice if you actually taught game theory instead of just giving your opinions and  being like, "I used game theory to form these conclusions. so listening to these opinions is learning game theory."

For example, we haven't even encountered one of the most basic tools of game theory—expected value. Can't say you're teaching game theory if you haven't introduced ideas as basic as expected value or probability.

Another basis of game theory—without which, you can't even engage in game theory—are the basic operations of conditional probability. Like the probability of event A happening given that condition B is true.  The fancy name is called Bayesian probability or logic for any of those pedantic people out there.

So yeah, it would be nice to learn some actual game theory and how to apply it to current events. If you want to teach actual game theory, you should restructure your teaching plan to include those basic ideas. I could use a refresher, and that's what I was hoping to get from your videos dammit.  At least working through the prisoner's dillema, just so people can learn how to use the basic tools.

> For some reason, I found that what you said was so backwards that I came to the possible argument that "game theory is rooted in empiricism". At this point, given that I'm just a layman and don't actually usually use words like "empiricism", I asked AI, and this is what it told me:  
>   
> --  
> Game theory is not inherently an empirical theory; rather, it is primarily a branch of mathematics used to model interactive decision-making, where its empirical status depends on the interpretive framework applied. 
  
> 
  
> Under the Revealed Preference Theory (RPT) interpretation, championed by Paul Samuelson, game theory is viewed as a set of mathematical tools to model entities that maximize utility functions, making it a tautology that cannot be refuted by empirical observations.  In this view, the assumption that agents act rationally is part of the definition of an "agent" rather than an empirical claim about human psychology. 
  
> 
  
> However, when game theory is applied as a descriptive account of human behavior, it relies on empiricism to test its predictions against real-world data.  Experimental economics has shown that while qualitative predictions often map onto observed behavior in economics, political science, and biology, empirical validity varies significantly based on model assumptions.  Empirical success improves when models incorporate realistic information, bounded cognition, and social preferences, but deviations are common when relying on strong rationality and common knowledge assumptions. 
  
> 
  
> Mathematical Foundation: At its core, game theory provides rules for symbolic manipulation to simplify representations of strategic situations, similar to arithmetic. 
  
> Empirical Testing: Laboratory experiments, such as the ultimatum and dictator games, reveal that human behavior often violates standard game-theoretic predictions, particularly regarding self-interest and "other-regarding" preferences. 
  
> Evolutionary Applications: Evolutionary game theory successfully explains regularities in biology, such as frequency-dependent selection and cooperation mechanisms, providing strong empirical support in that specific domain.  
>    
> -- end of AI input.  
>   
> As you can see, on its second paragraph, the AI assistant highlighted that game theory actually "relies on empiricism to test its predictions against real-world data", which is kind of what I was saying, and the opposite of what you were saying.

### **@KilotonFun**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=Ugz16xajHxC0n2sE9Gp4AaABAg)

If you never ran day three, then you're getting a skewed reading. 

You're avoiding the very thing that makes game theory deep, continual repeated games.

Playing the game once or even twice creates different pressures that effect the game. Is this a Bayesian game? A dynamic game? I understand the time constraints, but this isn't game theory, it's Cargo Cult game theory. 

Yes, I understand the thesis is that the least wins out. But what's the strategy then of the winner? Do they even need a strategy? Also, WHY AREN'T WE TALKING ABOUT STRATAGIES? If it's really game theory, what were the day to day relationships in terms of actual game theory stratagies? Were they forgiving of other nations? Did they totally comply or did they say no? When they were weak, would the openly supply mercanaries to both sidea of a conflict? What sort of leverage in terms of power did the rising nation need in order to begin making its own demands?

It sounds like we’re labeling actors ‘least likely’ after they win, which risks survivorship bias. How does the model avoid that?

Game theory doesn’t predict that low-probability actors win.
It predicts that actors with incentive-compatible strategies under specific constraints can outperform materially stronger players.

I subscribe to the same model of empires rising and falling as the professor, but I would not agree with his conclusion on the three emerging powers.

Israel is simply not cohesive, and is indeed deeply corrupt rather than energetic. Israeli society is in reality deeply divided, with the hardcore Zionists being heavily opposed by the rest of the Jewish population for whom the very concept of Zionism is heresy (it is the Messiah, not secular men like Ben-Gurion or Netanyahu, who are supposed to restore the state of Israel). That Israel is at war with basically all of its neighbors at this point is indeed a consequence of elite overproduction and the Israeli corruption of the American political system - as the wars are meant to keep Netanyahu and the Zionist wing in power.

It can be argued to be more open and creative, but even this tends to be doubtful when you get past the propaganda and realize that Israel's much vaunted technological superiority is entirely dependent on America. Contrary to popular belief Israeli weapons are not better than America's, and have not been for a while now. The Israeli F-35 for example is touted as having extra features making it better than the American version, but in reality the Israeli F-35s are almost entirely made in the US, with only the wing tips custom-manufactured in Israel.

By contrast Japan was able to re-make the F-15 almost entirely within Japan using their own components, and this threatened US manufacturers so much that they enforced Japan buying US components for the F-2 and F-35; and yet even this hasn't stopped Japan from developing its own fighter aircraft.

Japan and Germany's problems are thus less pronounced as Israel, but they still have cohesion and corruption issues due to their vassal state status. Quite simply, too many of the ruling class are still tethered to the US Empire; but at least they are not as completely reliant on it as Israel.

The more likely winners in a post-US world are either Iran or Turkey in the Middle East (with the latter having less cohesion issues, and are far more open), one of the East European states in Europe (Poland), and a Southeast Asian state (eg Vietnam). Japan and Germany frankly feel too tied to the old empire and share its corrupting slide, whereas Israel is basically integral to the American Empire.

### **@tminusmat**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ybufqRY77PQ&lc=UgwjKWkQPuPNgR4t4KF4AaABAg)

People love saying “competition drives innovation” like war is a startup incubator and collapse is a TED Talk. That’s not what’s happening.

What competition really does—what warring states really do—is blow a hole in the old veto structure. It doesn’t magically create new ideas. It removes the people who were blocking them. Most “innovations” were already sitting there the whole time: tried somewhere, whispered about, held by weirdos, monks, artisans, philosophers, and marginalized schools that never got invited into policy because things were “going fine.” When the system is stable, leadership acts conservative. Not because they’re stupid—because stability rewards staying in the lane. New ideas are risk. Risk threatens the hierarchy. So the hierarchy shelves them.

Then legitimacy breaks. The old stories stop working. The rituals don’t calm anyone down. The economy stops giving people patience. The crowd gets loud. Suddenly the rulers can’t govern by tradition and vibes anymore. They need something that actually reorganizes behavior, re-centers meaning, and cools the population without pure violence. So they go rummaging through the closet of “ideas we ignored for decades” and act like they just invented them.

That’s why the Warring States looks like a philosophical fireworks show. Daoism, Confucianism, Mohism didn’t pop into existence because everybody was fighting. They got adopted because the old mandate narrative cracked and the rulers needed new ways to coordinate people. Same thing in Greece—there were rhetoricians and thinkers before Socrates. The crisis didn’t create thought; it created demand for thought that could stabilize a city-state that was eating itself.

Buddhism fits this perfectly. It doesn’t require a calm empire to exist. It spreads when suffering becomes undeniable and the old authority stories don’t explain it anymore. It offers an interior technology for reducing violence, disciplining desire, and restoring coherence without needing a king to crack skulls every time people panic. That’s not “war creates progress.” That’s “when the old script fails, people finally try the script that was already on the shelf.”

So yeah—competition doesn’t create innovation. It just makes the gatekeepers desperate enough to stop pretending they’re too important to learn.
